Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I just need to do something with my hands, like it feels uncomfortable to just… stay still. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Like during sprint planning at work, or even on mortgage calls, I’ll know I should just sit there and focus, but I’ll suddenly get up to get water or check something in another room.\n\nIt sounds kind of minor when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you get up during these situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can look pretty calm, I hope, but inside it feels like this pressure building, like I need to move or switch to something else or at least do something with my hands. It’s not dramatic, but it can feel kind of like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to move happen in other situations besides work and mortgage calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does. Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.

Like, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.

Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.

I mean, I can look pretty calm, I hope, but inside it feels like this pressure building, like I need to move or switch to something else or at least do something with my hands. It’s not dramatic, but it can feel kind of like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to move happen in other situations besides work and mortgage calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does. Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I just need to do something with my hands, like it feels uncomfortable to just… stay still. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. So I can lose time just figuring out, “Wait, what was I supposed to do next?” instead of actually doing it.\n\nI can usually pull it together eventually, but it takes more effort than I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly missing appointments or anything like that. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.\n\nI can usually bring myself back, but it’s kind of embarrassing how many times I have to reread the same thing. Does that count as difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something on impulse that had serious consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call serious consequences. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I’ve been noticing I can’t always stay seated when I’m supposed to. Like during sprint planning at work, or even on mortgage calls, I’ll know I should just sit there and focus, but I’ll suddenly get up to get water or check something in another room.\n\nIt sounds kind of minor when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you get up during these situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can look pretty calm, I hope, but inside it feels like this pressure building, like I need to move or switch to something else or at least do something with my hands. It’s not dramatic, but it can feel kind of like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to move happen in other situations besides work and mortgage calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does. Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I just need to do something with my hands, like it feels uncomfortable to just… stay still. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. So I can lose time just figuring out, “Wait, what was I supposed to do next?” instead of actually doing it.\n\nI can usually pull it together eventually, but it takes more effort than I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly missing appointments or anything like that. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.\n\nI can usually bring myself back, but it’s kind of embarrassing how many times I have to reread the same thing. Does that count as difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something on impulse that had serious consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call serious consequences. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as restlessness or impulsivity, significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but… yes, it has started to interfere some.\n\nAt work and at home, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d handle. I’m trying really hard, so it feels a little uncomfortable to admit, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely creating friction.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven’t been fired or anything like that.

I guess the honest answer is there have been some problems at work, but I’d describe them as friction more than something major. People have had to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d take care of. I do worry a little that I’m starting to seem like the unreliable one, even though I really am trying.

So… not “significant” like losing a job, but it is affecting things some.

Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. So I can lose time just figuring out, “Wait, what was I supposed to do next?” instead of actually doing it.\n\nI can usually pull it together eventually, but it takes more effort than I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly missing appointments or anything like that. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.\n\nI can usually bring myself back, but it’s kind of embarrassing how many times I have to reread the same thing. Does that count as difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something on impulse that had serious consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call serious consequences. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as restlessness or impulsivity, significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but… yes, it has started to interfere some.\n\nAt work and at home, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d handle. I’m trying really hard, so it feels a little uncomfortable to admit, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely creating friction.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems at work because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been fired or anything like that.\n\nI guess the honest answer is there have been some problems at work, but I’d describe them as friction more than something major. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as restlessness or impulsivity, significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but… yes, it has started to interfere some.\n\nAt work and at home, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d handle. I’m trying really hard, so it feels a little uncomfortable to admit, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely creating friction.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems at work because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been fired or anything like that.\n\nI guess the honest answer is there have been some problems at work, but I’d describe them as friction more than something major. People have had to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d take care of. I do worry a little that I’m starting to seem like the unreliable one, even though I really am trying.\n\nSo… not “significant” like losing a job, but it is affecting things some.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave your symptoms caused any problems with your daily life or relationships that you would describe as more than mild?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / software_developer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Functional Impairment\nmanifestation: \"It has started creating friction at work and at home because people have to follow up with me more than they used to, and I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks even though I’m trying hard.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… maybe more than mild, but I feel a little hesitant saying that because it’s not like everything is falling apart.

It’s more that it’s started creating friction, both at work and at home. People have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning things or tasks I said I’d handle. And I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations, even though I really am trying hard.

So yes, I guess I’d say it’s more than mild in that sense.

I mean, I can look pretty calm, I hope, but inside it feels like this pressure building, like I need to move or switch to something else or at least do something with my hands. It’s not dramatic, but it can feel kind of like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to move happen in other situations besides work and mortgage calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does. Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it does get to me, more than I’d like to admit.

I try to tell myself it’s not that bad, because I’m still functioning and I’m really trying. But when people at work or at home have to keep following up with me about things I said I’d handle, it feels embarrassing. Especially with planning tasks—I start worrying that I’m becoming the unreliable one.

So yes, I do feel distressed by it, even if I’m also trying not to make it sound worse than it is.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um… I guess what finally pushed me to make the appointment is that I’ve been noticing I can’t always stay seated when I’m supposed to. Like during sprint planning at work, or even on mortgage calls, I’ll know I should just sit there and focus, but I’ll suddenly get up to get water or check something in another room.\n\nIt sounds kind of minor when I say it out loud, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should ask about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel restless when you get up during these situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I can look pretty calm, I hope, but inside it feels like this pressure building, like I need to move or switch to something else or at least do something with my hands. It’s not dramatic, but it can feel kind of like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just sit there.\n\nDoctor: Does this feeling of restlessness and need to move happen in other situations besides work and mortgage calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it does. Not all the time, and I don’t want to overstate it, but even in normal sitting situations I can look like I’m being calm, while inside there’s this kind of pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I just need to do something with my hands, like it feels uncomfortable to just… stay still. Like I’m going to crawl out of my skin a little, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience feelings of restlessness or difficulty staying still when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was constantly disruptive or anything, but I do remember in elementary school teachers sometimes moving my desk or reminding me to stay seated.\n\nI was kind of always popping up, or I’d talk before I was called on. And I remember rushing through work even when I understood it, which probably wasn’t ideal. I guess at the time I thought I was just being eager or a little restless, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Did you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes in your work or school assignments?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t immediately think of myself as someone who makes a lot of careless mistakes, at least not in a way that stands out to me.\n\nI mean, everyone misses details sometimes, but I don’t know that I’d say it was often or a major pattern. If you mean like consistently overlooking instructions or turning things in full of errors, I don’t really have a clear memory of that.\n\nDoctor: Did you often interrupt others or blurt out answers before you were called on in school?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. I wouldn’t want to make it sound like I was trying to be rude, but I do remember blurting things out or answering before I was actually called on.\n\nEven now I catch myself doing a version of that at work, where I’ll start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then sometimes I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is… embarrassing. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. So I can lose time just figuring out, “Wait, what was I supposed to do next?” instead of actually doing it.\n\nI can usually pull it together eventually, but it takes more effort than I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly missing appointments or anything like that. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.\n\nI can usually bring myself back, but it’s kind of embarrassing how many times I have to reread the same thing. Does that count as difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something on impulse that had serious consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call serious consequences. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as restlessness or impulsivity, significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but… yes, it has started to interfere some.\n\nAt work and at home, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d handle. I’m trying really hard, so it feels a little uncomfortable to admit, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely creating friction.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems at work because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been fired or anything like that.\n\nI guess the honest answer is there have been some problems at work, but I’d describe them as friction more than something major. People have had to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d take care of. I do worry a little that I’m starting to seem like the unreliable one, even though I really am trying.\n\nSo… not “significant” like losing a job, but it is affecting things some.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused any problems with your daily life or relationships that you would describe as more than mild?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more than mild, but I feel a little hesitant saying that because it’s not like everything is falling apart.\n\nIt’s more that it’s started creating friction, both at work and at home. People have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning things or tasks I said I’d handle. And I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations, even though I really am trying hard.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’d say it’s more than mild in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt distressed by your symptoms, like they're really getting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get to me, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s not that bad, because I’m still functioning and I’m really trying. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. I try to catch it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others or blurt out things in social situations, like when you're with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially I try pretty hard not to, because I don’t want to come across as rude. With friends or family it probably happens a little, but the place I notice it most is honestly at work.\n\nLike, I’ll catch myself answering a teammate before they’ve even finished explaining the bug or the question. And sometimes I’m already talking when I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is kind of embarrassing. I don’t mean to cut people off, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without much consideration for the consequences?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe in a mild way? I don’t think of myself as someone who does big impulsive things without caring about consequences, but I do notice it in conversations.\n\nLike at work, I’ll sometimes start answering a teammate before they’ve finished explaining the bug or the question. And then I’m already talking and realize I assumed where they were going, and I guessed wrong. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I try not to do it, but it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, though I’ve tried to make systems for it, so I don’t know if it counts as “often” in the worst way.\n\nIt’s more that things end up scattered. Like my work tickets might be partly in Slack, then I have sticky notes, emails I marked unread, reminders on my phone… and then home repair notes or budget reminders are in some other place. So I can lose time just figuring out, “Wait, what was I supposed to do next?” instead of actually doing it.\n\nI can usually pull it together eventually, but it takes more effort than I feel like it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you frequently forget things you were supposed to do, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I don’t think I’m someone who’s constantly missing appointments or anything like that. I try pretty hard to use reminders, calendars, that kind of thing.\n\nTasks, maybe sometimes, but it’s hard to say if that’s “frequent.” I’ll occasionally realize I meant to do something and it slipped, but I don’t know if it’s more than normal. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you when you're trying to focus on a task?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “often,” exactly. I mean, I can get distracted sometimes, especially if there’s a lot going on around me, but I don’t know if it’s more than what other people deal with.\n\nIf I’m really trying to focus, I usually put on headphones or try to block things out, so maybe I’ve gotten used to managing it. But… yeah, I’m not totally sure how to judge that. Does that sound like what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty sustaining focus during tasks that require prolonged mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that one does happen, though I don’t know how severe it sounds.\n\nLike, I’ll sit down to review a pull request or read something important like mortgage documents, and I start with good intentions. But after a few minutes I’ll realize I’m just staring at the same paragraph or same section, and my brain is off thinking about five unrelated things.\n\nI can usually bring myself back, but it’s kind of embarrassing how many times I have to reread the same thing. Does that count as difficulty sustaining focus?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever done something on impulse that had serious consequences?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call serious consequences. I mean, I’ve made quick decisions before and later thought, “Okay, that wasn’t the best,” but not like something that caused major damage or really big fallout.\n\nAt least nothing is coming to mind. If you mean a specific kind of consequence, I can think about it that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had problems in your personal relationships due to acting on impulse?\n\nPatient: Maybe… yes, but I wouldn’t want to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nIt’s more like it’s started creating some friction at home and at work because people have to follow up with me more than they used to. Like with planning things or tasks I said I’d handle, I’ll jump in and agree or move too quickly, and then someone has to circle back and check whether I actually did it or what the plan is.\n\nI feel bad saying that, because I really am trying, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning stuff. I don’t know if that’s exactly what you mean by relationship problems, but it has affected how people respond to me a bit.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms, such as restlessness or impulsivity, significantly interfered with your ability to perform your job or maintain relationships?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say it’s been disastrous or anything, but… yes, it has started to interfere some.\n\nAt work and at home, people have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d handle. I’m trying really hard, so it feels a little uncomfortable to admit, but I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations.\n\nI don’t know if that counts as “significant,” but it’s definitely creating friction.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been fired from a job or had significant problems at work because of your restlessness, impulsivity, or difficulty staying focused?\n\nPatient: No, I haven’t been fired or anything like that.\n\nI guess the honest answer is there have been some problems at work, but I’d describe them as friction more than something major. People have had to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning or tasks I said I’d take care of. I do worry a little that I’m starting to seem like the unreliable one, even though I really am trying.\n\nSo… not “significant” like losing a job, but it is affecting things some.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused any problems with your daily life or relationships that you would describe as more than mild?\n\nPatient: I think… maybe more than mild, but I feel a little hesitant saying that because it’s not like everything is falling apart.\n\nIt’s more that it’s started creating friction, both at work and at home. People have to follow up with me more than they used to, especially around planning things or tasks I said I’d handle. And I do worry I’m becoming the unreliable one in those situations, even though I really am trying hard.\n\nSo yes, I guess I’d say it’s more than mild in that sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt distressed by your symptoms, like they're really getting to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does get to me, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nI try to tell myself it’s not that bad, because I’m still functioning and I’m really trying. But when people at work or at home have to keep following up with me about things I said I’d handle, it feels embarrassing. Especially with planning tasks—I start worrying that I’m becoming the unreliable one.\n\nSo yes, I do feel distressed by it, even if I’m also trying not to make it sound worse than it is.\n\nDoctor: Would you describe your symptoms as causing significant problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: I’d say… yes, but maybe not “significant” in the most severe sense, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more that it’s started creating friction in my day-to-day life, at work and at home. People have to follow up with me more than they used to about things I said I’d handle, especially planning-related tasks, and that’s embarrassing. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one, even though I really am trying hard.\n\nSo yes, I think it is causing problems—just not, you know, catastrophic ones.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
